Let me understand the setup. This is a System F (locally nameless) benchmark. I need to prove a parametricity free theorem: any closed term of type `forall X, X -> X`, applied to a closed value `v : U`, evaluates to `v`.

Let me check what Rocq version is installed, and look around the directory.

Let me think about the proof strategy. The standard approach is a logical relation:
- `R_U(v)` for closed value v of type U.
- Term-level relation for type `Ty_BVar` etc.

A simpler approach: define a logical relation `R : ty -> tm -> Prop` (unary, "value v is in the interpretation of T" where T is closed). Then fundamental theorem: if `has_type [] empty t T` and `lc_ty T` (i.e., T closed) then `R T t`... but t may not be a value; need the relation to be on terms: define `R T t` as "t is in the interpretation" where for arrow types it means for all values u with R T1 u, tm_app t u -->* something in R T2. For all types: if R T t then ... Actually with nondeterminism, we need the relation to be closed under expansion: if t -->* t' and R t' then R t (where appropriate). Standard "logical relation with step-index" or the usual "relation is closed under backward reduction".

Actually with a binary relation, `t U v -->* v`.

Hmm wait. The key point: For `t : ∀X. Actually simpler: define the type interpretation relative to a list `Rs : list rel` (for BVars) and `ρ : atom -> rel` (for fvars):

```
Fixpoint Rty (Rs : list rel) (rho : atom -> rel) (T : ty) : rel :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => nth i Rs (fun _ _ => True)  (* only closed types are ever used *)
  | Ty_FVar X => rho X
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => arrow_rel (Rty Rs rho T1) (Rty Rs rho T2)
  | Ty_All T1 => forall_rel (fun X R => Rty (R :: Rs) (rho[X:=R]) T1)
  end
```

where arrow_rel and forall_rel are defined appropriately, and forall_rel quantifies over relations R and atoms X? Hmm, since type variables are named by atoms (fvars), the universal quantifier should quantify over a fresh atom X and a relation R, and extend rho. The de Bruijn list Rs handles BVar references: in `Rty (R :: Rs) (rho[X:=R]) T1`, BVar 0 gets R. But wait: our `open_ty T (Ty_FVar X)` replaces BVar 0 with Ty_FVar X. If we define the relation at `Ty_All T1` via `Rty [] rho (Ty_All T1) = forall X R, Rty [R] (rho[X:=R]) T1`, then this should equal `Rty [] (rho[X:=R]) (open_ty T1 (Ty_FVar X))`. That requires a substitution lemma: `Rty (R::Rs) rho' T1 = Rty Rs rho' (open_ty T1 (Ty_FVar X))` when rho' X = R. Since open_ty replaces BVar 0 with Ty_FVar X, and Rty maps BVar 0 to R and Ty_FVar X to rho' X = R, this works *if* the BVar indices in T1 are correctly handled: open_ty_rec also shifts? Wait, open_ty_rec doesn't shift indices; it replaces BVar k with U at the binder depth k. For T1 with free BVar 0, at depth 0 it becomes U. Under an inner Ty_All, depth k=1, BVar 1 becomes U, and BVar 0 (bound by inner) stays. So open_ty is a *simultaneous* substitution of the free variable 0 (de Bruijn index relative to each binder). Meanwhile Rty (R::Rs) rho' T1: BVar 0 → R (corresponding to X), and under inner Ty_All, BVar 1 → Rs[0] = R. Consistent. Good.

Actually here's a cleaner way to think: binary relation R on terms defined as `R(a,b) := a -->* v ∧ b -->* v`. The parametricity theorem instantiated with this R gives the result. The general binary relation is needed because the universal type in t can be instantiated with *any* relation, and inside t, the type variable X can appear in nested ways (e.g., X → X → X, or (X→X)→X), and the relation for those compound types must be built from R. Since R is a binary relation on terms, the compound relations are binary too. So yes, binary LR.

This is the standard binary fundamental theorem: for all Δ Γ t T, if has_type Δ Γ t T, then for all ρ, σ1, σ2 such that σ1 ~_Γ σ2 (i.e., for each (x,A) ∈ Γ, T_A(σ1 x, σ2 x)), we have T_T(σ1(t), σ2(t)) where T_T is the term relation.

Let's think about how the standard proof goes in a named representation with the "T_TAbs: for all X not in L, body has type T[X]" rule. The logical relation is often defined as:
- `R_{∀X.T}(v1,v2) := forall (U1 U2 : ty) (R : rel), R_{T[X:=U1]}(... v1 U1, ... v2 U2)` where the relation at type T with a *type environment* for fvars is used.

In a named representation, the premise gives us `body : T[X]` where X is an fvar, and the semantic environment maps X to a relation R. To prove the universal relation, we take arbitrary types U1, U2 and relation R, and we need to relate `(tabs body) U1` and `(tabs body) U2`, which step to `body[X:=U1]` and `body[X:=U2]`. The IH gives the relation at `T[X]` with ρ X = R: `R_{T[X]}(body, body)`. We need `R_{T[U1]}(body[X:=U1], body[X:=U2])`. There's a standard lemma "the logical relation is invariant under type substitution": if ρ maps X to the relation R (which relates terms of type U1 and U2), then `R_{T[X]}(body) ⟺ R_{T[U1,U2]}(body[X:=U1], body[X:=U2])`. This is the "semantic type substitution lemma" and it's usually proven by induction on T, using the fact that the term relation at arrow types doesn't inspect types and the universal case quantifies over all further type instantiations. Hmm, hold on. Let's go back to the standard parametricity proof in textbooks (e.g., Reynolds). For System F, the logical relation is defined on *typed* terms via a type interpretation. The proof of the "abstraction" case uses a lemma about type substitution: the interpretation `[[T]]` with type environment η satisfies `[[T[U/X]]]η = [[T]]η[X↦[[U]]η]`. And crucially, the *terms* are considered modulo typing: `t : T[U/X]` and the abstraction's body `t' : T[X]` instantiated... In the standard proof (e.g., in the "Theorems for Free!" paper), the relation at ∀ is defined as: `(v1,v2) ∈ [[∀X.T]]η` iff for all types U1,U2 and relations R ⊆ [[U1]]×[[U2]], the pair (v1 U1, v2 U2) ∈ [[T]]η[X↦(U1,U2,R)]. And the semantic substitution lemma is proven by induction on T *for the interpretations as relations*, where at a type variable X the environment gives the relation, and the syntactic substitution T[U/X] is handled because the environment at X *is* the interpretation of U. The key: the lemma is about the *relation* [[T[U/X]]]η = [[T]]η[X↦(U1,U2,R)], where U1, U2 are the types and R their relation. But the *terms* v1 U1 etc. are just argument applications. The lemma is purely about relations, not about particular terms, and it holds because the relations at type variables match! Let me check the arrow case: `[[A→B]]η = { (f1,f2) | forall (a1,a2) ∈ [[A]]η, (f1 a1, f2 a2) ∈ [[B]]η }`. With substitution: `[[(A→B)[U/X]]]η = [[A[U/X]→B[U/X]]]η = { (f1,f2) | forall (a1,a2) ∈ [[A[U/X]]]η, (f1 a1, f2 a2) ∈ [[B[U/X]]]η }`. By IH these are the same relations. ✓. The rational part: the relation is on terms, and the same terms f1, f2 are used on both sides. It works because the relation [[T[U/X]]] is compared to [[T]]η[X↦...] as *relations on the same untyped term space*, and the recursion only substitutes in types, not in terms. The term components (f1 a1) are identical.

So in our setting, the type-substitution lemma is: `Rty Rs ρ (open_ty T U) = Rty (Rty [] ρ U :: Rs) ρ T` as relations (functions tm -> tm -> Prop), and this is provable by induction on T *without touching terms*! The `Ty_All` case: LHS `Rty Rs ρ (open_ty (Ty_All T2) U) = Rty Rs ρ (Ty_All (open_ty_rec 1 U T2))` = `fun t1 t2 => forall X R, Rty (R::Rs) ρ (open_ty_rec 1 U T2) (tapp t1 X, tapp t2 X)`. RHS `Rty (R_U::Rs) ρ (Ty_All T2) = fun t1 t2 => forall X R, Rty (R::R_U::Rs) ρ T2 (tapp t1 X, tapp t2 X)`. By IH (generalized at depth 1): `Rty (R::Rs) ρ (open_ty_rec 1 U T2) = Rty (R::R_U::Rs) ρ T2`. ✓. The terms `tapp t1 X` are identical on both sides! Great — no term mismatch, because the universal relation's term instantiation at the fvar X is the *same syntax* for both.

So the type substitution lemma works at the level of relations, with the *same* term. That's the key. Now the T_TApp case:

Let me look at this from the standard "logical predicate" for System F in the literature with locally nameless representations. In the locally nameless style, the semantic type relation is usually defined so that the universal case is:

`R_{∀α.T}(ρ)(v1, v2) := ∀ (α1 α2 : atom) (T1 T2 : ty) (R : rel), ρ' = ρ[α ↦ (T1,T2,R)]... ` hmm.

Actually, here's the standard trick in locally nameless: the type abstraction's premise gives a judgment for a *fresh fvar* α. The semantic environment for the type variable α is a pair of *closed types* (T1,T2) and a relation R between them. The body term is opened at α; to relate it at the fvar α, the type substitution is "α ↦ T1 on the left, α ↦ T2 on the right". So the semantic type environment maps each type variable to a pair (T1, T2, R), and the relation `R_T(ρ)` is a binary relation between terms, where the types on the two sides can differ! The type-substitution lemma then has a clean form: `R_{T[α:=U1,U2]}(ρ)(s1,s2)` where the left term s1 has type T[U1] and the right s2 has type T[U2]... and the lemma states `R_{T[α:=U1,U2]}(ρ \ α) = R_T(ρ[α ↦ (U1,U2,R)])`. But what are the terms? In the lemma, the relation on the left is at type T[U1] on the left side and T[U2] on the right side. The relation itself is determined by the type T and the environment. So for a term s with free type variable α, the relation `R_T(ρ[α↦(U1,U2,R)])` applied to (s, s)? Hmm, s is the same term on both sides, but its type is T[α] which semantically means T[U1] on the left and T[U2] on the right? But s's syntactic type annotations contain α, which would need to be substituted to U1 on the left and U2 on the right... but s is the same term. Hmm, in the semantic relation, the term s is untyped, so the same term can be considered as having different types on the left and right. The relation is defined on untyped terms, so `R_T(ρ[α↦(U1,U2,R)])(s,s)` is well-defined but the "type" of s isn't enforced. The type-substitution lemma then would be about relating different syntactic terms: `R_{T[U1/U2]}(s[α:=U1], s[α:=U2])`... Hmm, still terms differ.

Let me think about the type-substitution lemma in a way that's provable. Claim: define `R_T(ρ)` for T with type variables interpreted by ρ : atom -> (ty * ty * rel) — triples of left-type, right-type, relation. The lemma: `R_T(ρ)(s1, s2) ↔ R_{T[α:=U1,U2]}(ρ \ α)(s1, s2)` where T[α:=U1,U2] means substitute α by U1 on the left and U2 on the right... but types are single syntactic objects, so what does this mean? Right — in the two-sided setting, there are two types TL and TR and the relation at T is between terms of TL and TR. The substitution lemma: if ρ α = (U1,U2,R), and TL = T[U1/α], TR = T[U2/α], then `R_T(ρ)(s1,s2)` where s1 is "of type TL" and s2 "of type TR". The relation `R_T(ρ)` is defined by recursion on T:
- T = α: R_T = ρ α = R. ✓ (and the types U1, U2 are irrelevant to the relation itself).
- T = β ≠ α: ρ β = (V1,V2,Rβ), types V1,V2.
- T = A→B: R_T(s1,s2) := forall a1 a2, R_A(a1,a2) -> R_B(s1 a1, s2 a2). ✓ — no type inspection, so the substitution in types doesn't affect the relation, because the relation doesn't depend on types at the arrow case except recursively.
- T = ∀β.A: R_T(s1,s2) := forall β' (W1 W2 : ty) (R' : rel), R_A(ρ[β'↦(W1,W2,R')])(s1 W1, s2 W2). The types W1,W2 here are chosen by the quantifier. Under the substitution α:=U1/U2, the body A[α:=U1,U2] and the environment agree. Induction: R_A(ρ[β'↦...]) vs R_{A[U1,U2]}(ρ\α[β'↦...]) — the IH applies with the extended environment. ✓.

So the two-sided relation is invariant under type substitution *as a relation on untyped terms*, with the SAME terms s1, s2. So `R_T(ρ[α↦(U1,U2,R)])(s1,s2)` is what we get, and it doesn't matter what the types are! The relation is completely type-blind in the sense that its definition never inspects term type annotations or type arguments (except the ones it introduces itself at universal quantification).

NOW: in the typing derivation, the term `open_tm_ty t α` for fresh α has type `open_ty T1 α`. In the *semantic* relation for the variable α, we interpret types: the left side type is U1, right U2, relation R. The body term at α is `open_tm_ty t α` — a syntactic term containing α in its type annotations. The relation `R_{open_ty T1 α}(ρ[α↦(U1,U2,R)])` applied to `(open_tm_ty t α, open_tm_ty t α)`. By the type-substitution lemma, this equals `R_{open_ty T1 U1, open_ty T1 U2}(ρ)(...)` with the same terms. Hmm, but wait: the substitution lemma says subbing α by U1/U2 in the TYPE doesn't change the relation, with the same terms. Since the relation on the term doesn't inspect types, and the terms are the same, we get: the relation at the opened type (with α at U1/U2) applied to the α-opened term is the same as ... hmm, we want the relation at the U-instantiated type applied to the U-instantiated term. The terms `open_tm_ty t α` vs `open_tm_ty t U` still differ.

BUT: here's the key — maybe we don't need to change the term! In the T_TApp soundness case, the actual term is `tm_tapp t U`. Hmm, but for T_TAbs, the goal is the relation at `∀X.T1` applied to `tm_tabs t`, whose universal definition applies it to types W1, W2 giving `tm_tapp (tm_tabs t) W1` and `tm_tapp (tm_tabs t) W2`; these step to `open_tm_ty t W1` and `open_tm_ty t W2`. So the U-version term arises from the operational semantics. The IH gives the α-version. So we need to relate `open_tm_ty t W1` to `open_tm_ty t α`. Hmm, unless the universal relation's definition is arranged so that the terms used are the α-versions with α interpreted as W1/W2? I.e., what if we define the universal relation to apply the type abstraction to a *fresh fvar* α, but with the type environment giving α the pair (W1,W2,R)? Then `tm_tapp (tm_tabs t) α --> open_tm_ty t α`, which is exactly the term the IH is about! But then the relation at `open_ty T1 α` with α at (W1,W2,R) is, by the substitution lemma, the same relation as at `open_ty T1 W1`/`open_ty T1 W2` with the same term. But the term `tm_tapp (tm_tabs t) α` has type `open_ty T1 α`, semantically T1[W1] on the left, T1[W2] on the right — but the actual term after reduction, `open_tm_ty t α`, is a term whose type annotations mention α, not W1/W2. Semantically we're free to assign it the "type" T1[W1]/T1[W2] because the relation is type-blind! That's the beauty of the two-sided untyped relation: the term's syntactic type annotations don't matter; the relation is defined syntactically on untyped terms and the typing derivation only ensures well-typedness/reduction properties.

Hold on, is that right? The relation at the universal type is defined abstractly on term pairs; the definition doesn't require the terms to have the right type. The fundamental lemma proves that well-typed terms are in the relation. The relation's definition at ∀ quantifies over W1,W2,R and applies the terms to W1,W2 via `tm_tapp`. The relation at the result type is defined on *untyped pairs*. So the fact that `open_tm_ty t α` has syntactic type `open_ty T1 α` doesn't constrain the semantics; the relation at `T1[W1]/T1[W2]` applies regardless. And the substitution lemma gives that this relation equals the relation at the "α-version" of the type, which is exactly what the IH provides (since the typing judgment at α is what's given). 

So the key insight: work with the two-sided relation with type environments mapping fvars to triples (left type, right type, relation) — OR, in our same-type setting, pairs/types? Let's simplify: since ultimately both sides use the same type U and the same relation, but the *intermediate* type environment can have different left/right types (because the universal relation quantifies over W1, W2 independently). Also, our target: t : ∀X.X→X, instantiate at U on both sides with relation R_v. We need same-type at the end. But the general machinery needs two-sided.

Hmm, so let's consider doing the full two-sided binary logical relation with type environments mapping each fvar to a triple `(T1, T2, R)` of two closed types and a relation on terms. Wait, but do we even need the types in the environment? Let's revisit the substitution lemma with the environment mapping fvars to just relations R (no types), and the universal relation quantifying over closed types W1, W2 and relations R':

`Rty Rs ρ (Ty_All T1) := fun t1 t2 => forall W1 W2 R', Rty (R'::Rs) ρ T1 (tm_tapp t1 W1, tm_tapp t2 W2)`.

Wait — the two sides get different type instantiations W1, W2! Because the two sides of the relation can be terms "of different types". In our final application, both sides instantiate at the same U, but in general the ∀ relation quantifies over all pairs. Then:
- T_TApp soundness: `t : ∀X.T1`, `t : open_ty T1 U`. Hypothesis: forall W1 W2 R', relation at T1 (with R') of `(tapp (σ1 t) W1, tapp (σ2 t) W2)`. Goal: relation at `open_ty T1 U` of `(tapp (σ1 t) U, tapp (σ2 t) U)`. Taking W1 = W2 = U and R' := `Rty [] ρ U` (the interpretation of U — same-type relation for U on both sides), we get the goal by the type-substitution lemma. ✓ (Since both sides same U, no issue.)
- T_TAbs soundness: Goal: forall W1 W2 R', relation at T1 with R' of `(tapp (tabs (σ1 t)) W1, tapp (tabs (σ2 t)) W2)`. By the operational semantics, these step to `(open_tm_ty (σ1 t) W1, open_tm_ty (σ2 t) W2)`. The relation should be closed under backward/forward reduction. The IH at fresh α, with ρ' = ρ[α ↦ R']: `Rty [] ρ' (open_ty T1 α) (σ1(open_tm_ty t α), σ2(open_tm_ty t α))`. By the type-substitution lemma, `Rty [] ρ' (open_ty T1 α) = Rty (R'::Rs) ρ T1` *as relations on untyped terms with these same terms*. So IH gives `Rty (R'::Rs) ρ T1 (σ1(open_tm_ty t α), σ2(open_tm_ty t α))`.

But the goal is `Rty (R'::Rs) ρ T1 (σ1(open_tm_ty t W1), σ2(open_tm_ty t W2))` — the terms differ by α vs W1/W2 substitutions! Hmm!! Still the same problem. The type-substitution lemma keeps terms fixed; it changes the *type* but not the term. So the IH's terms have α in them; the goal's terms have W1/W2.

Damn. But wait — maybe the relation being "type-blind" means the terms `open_tm_ty t α` (with α as an fvar of type... interpreted as relation R') and the terms `open_tm_ty t W1`... hmm.

Hmm, unless we make the *step* go the other way: the goal term is `tm_tapp (tabs (σ t)) W1`, which steps to `open_tm_ty (σ t) W1`. The IH is at α. If instead we define the universal relation to use `tm_tapp t α` with α a fresh fvar, then the goal terms would match the IH exactly! And the T_TApp case would then need `tm_tapp t U` vs `tm_tapp t α` — an issue, but maybe there the types U and the relation Rty [] ρ U align differently. Let's define:

`Rty Rs ρ (Ty_All T1) := fun t1 t2 => forall (α : atom) (R' : rel), Rty (R'::Rs) ρ T1 (tm_tapp t1 (Ty_FVar α), tm_tapp t2 (Ty_FVar α))`.

Then T_TApp: hypothesis gives forall α R', relation at T1 (R') of (tapp (σ1 t) α, tapp (σ2 t) α). Goal: relation at open_ty T1 U of (tapp (σ1 t) U, tapp (σ2 t) U). By type-substitution lemma, relation at `open_ty T1 U` = relation at T1 with R' := Rty [] ρ U (as relations, same terms). So goal is `Rty (R_U::Rs) ρ T1 (tapp (σ1 t) U, tapp (σ2 t) U)`. Hypothesis gives α-version with R'. We need to equate the U-version. Since R' = R_U is the interpretation of the *type* U, and the terms `tapp (σ t) U` vs `tapp (σ t) α` with α interpreted by R_U... Now here's a thought: the type-substitution lemma in the *term* sense: is `Rty (R_U::Rs) ρ T1 (tapp s U, tapp s' U) ↔ Rty (R_U::Rs) ρ T1 (tapp s α, tapp s' α)` when ρ α = R_U? Hmm, the terms still differ (U vs α as type argument). But now, since R_U = Rty [] ρ U is the interpretation of U, maybe the relation at the type variable position... Let's think about the structure of `tapp s U`: the relation `Rty (R_U::Rs) ρ T1` applied to terms whose "type" is T1[U]. Whereas `tapp s α` has "type" T1[α] with α interpreted as R_U. The relation is type-blind, so both should be in the same relation if the terms have the same "structure modulo types". Formally, we need an invariance lemma: `Rty (R_U :: Rs) ρ T1 (s1, s2)` is invariant under replacing type occurrences in s1, s2 according to the substitution α→U, where ρ α = R_U.

Let me try to prove this by induction on T1 with the following precise statement. Define an operation `tsubst_tm : (atom -> ty) -> tm -> tm` replacing type fvars by types (in type annotations and type args). Claim: if σ is a type substitution (mapping atoms to types) such that for each atom α, `Rty [] ρ (σ α) = ρ α` (as relations), then for all T and terms s1,s2: `Rty Rs ρ T (s1,s2) ↔ Rty Rs ρ (T σ) (s1 σ, s2 σ)`. Hmm, at T = BVar: both sides are Rs! i. Need Rs i = Rs i ✓ (types don't matter). At T = FVar α: LHS ρ α; RHS ρ (σ α)? Wait the type after substitution is σ α, and the relation at type `σ α` is `Rty Rs ρ (σ α)`, which by assumption equals ρ α when σ α is closed. ✓. At arrow: LHS forall a1 a2, R_A(a1,a2) -> R_B(s1 a1, s2 a2); RHS forall a1 a2, R_{Aσ}(a1,a2) -> R_{Bσ}(s1σ a1, s2σ a2). The IH gives R_A(a1,a2) ↔ R_{Aσ}(a1σ, a2σ)... but here the inputs are the SAME terms a1,a2 on both sides, whereas IH relates A to Aσ with different terms. Hmm. Unless the claim is strengthened: for all terms s1,s2, `Rty Rs ρ T (s1,s2) ↔ Rty Rs ρ (Tσ) (s1σ, s2σ)` AND `Rty Rs ρ T (s1,s2) ↔ Rty Rs ρ (Tσ) (s1,s2)`? The second is false for arbitrary terms: if T = FVar α, LHS = ρ α, RHS = ρ (σ α), and terms are the same; assumption says these relations are equal. Then at arrow: RHS with same terms: forall a1 a2, R_{Aσ}(a1,a2) -> R_{Bσ}(s1 a1, s2 a2). Using IH in the "same terms" form: R_A(a1,a2) ↔ R_{Aσ}(a1,a2) [assuming the assumption about σ], and R_B(s1 a1, s2 a2) ↔ R_{Bσ}(s1 a1, s2 a2)? But the subterms `s1 a1` — hmm, IH (same-terms form) applied to the particular terms s1 a1, s2 a2: gives R_B(s1 a1,s2 a2) ↔ R_{Bσ}(s1 a1, s2 a2) ✓. So the same-terms form goes through at arrow, GIVEN the assumption that for all atoms α, ρ α = Rty Rs ρ (σ α).

At ∀: LHS `Rty Rs ρ (Ty_All T1)` = fun t1 t2 => forall α R', Rty (R'::Rs) ρ T1 (tapp t1 α, tapp t2 α) [fvar version]. RHS (same-terms form) `Rty Rs ρ (Ty_All (T1σ))` = fun t1 t2 => forall α R', Rty (R'::Rs) ρ (T1σ) (tapp t1 α, tapp t2 α). By IH (same-terms form, under extended environment R'::Rs and same ρ) ✓!! 

So the same-terms invariance holds: **the relation `Rty Rs ρ T` is invariant under type substitution σ in T, with the same terms, provided ρ α = Rty Rs ρ (σ α) for all atoms α in the domain** (and σ maps to closed types so that Rty is well-defined). 

Wait, but the assumption `ρ α = Rty Rs ρ (σ α)` must hold for the extended environments too, including when we go under ∀ and extend Rs with R'. Since Rty with a different BVar list applies to σ α which is closed (BVar-free), the Rs doesn't matter (closed types have no free BVars, so Rty Rs ρ (σ α) = Rty [] ρ (σ α)). Good, so the assumption is stable.

Now apply this to our situation! We want: `Rty (R_U::Rs) ρ T1 (tapp s1 U, tapp s2 U)` from `Rty (R_U::Rs) ρ T1 (tapp s1 α, tapp s2 α)`. Hmm, this is not exactly the same-terms invariance. Let's see: The same-terms invariance says `Rty (R_U::Rs) ρ T1 (t1,t2) ↔ Rty (R_U::Rs) ρ (T1 σ) (t1,t2)` for the same t1,t2. Setting t1 = tapp s1 U, t2 = tapp s2 U: RHS would be `Rty (R_U::Rs) ρ (T1 σ) (tapp s1 U, tapp s2 U)` — and if T1 σ = T1 (i.e., T1 has no fvars in σ's domain), we're stuck with the U-terms.

But here's the thing: in T_TApp, we have `T1` with BVar 0 and fvars from Δ. The hypothesis gives us, for all α, R': `Rty (R'::Rs) ρ T1 (tapp s1 α, tapp s2 α)`. We want the U-version with R' := Rty [] ρ U.

Hmm, what if we use the *term-level* invariance directly: the terms `tapp s U` vs `tapp s α` are related to each other by a type substitution on terms, and the relation at T1 with BVar0↦R_U should be invariant... but the relation `Rty (R_U::Rs) ρ T1` doesn't involve α or U syntactically; it's the terms that differ. The same-terms invariance lemma above says the relation is unchanged when we substitute in the TYPE, not the terms. To change the terms, we'd need a "term-level" version.

Hmm, wait, actually maybe we can leverage the definitional structure: `Rty (R_U::Rs) ρ T1 (tapp s1 U, ...)`. The relation is defined by recursion on T1. At each step, it applies the terms to related pairs. Since the relation doesn't inspect types in the terms, the difference between `tapp s1 U` and `tapp s1 α` matters only when s1's structure is exposed through applications. Hmm, but the relation *does* apply `tm_tapp` at universal types: at type `Ty_All T2`, the relation applies `tm_tapp t2 α'` for arbitrary α'. If the term is `tapp s1 U` and the type T1's head is Ty_All, then we apply `tm_tapp (tapp s1 U) α'`. Since `tapp s1 U` has a type argument U whereas the α-version has α. Hmm.

I think the cleanest fix is to make the typing rule's fvar-based opening correspond to the semantics' fvar-based quantification, and handle T_TApp by a *term-level substitution lemma proven by induction on the term*, which seems hard.

Hmm, but wait: the type substitution σ maps fvars to types. In the fundamental lemma, the typing derivation has type context Δ. The fvars in Δ get types via σ1, σ2. Actually, wait. Let me reconsider the problem. The issue was relating `open_tm_ty t X` to `open_tm_ty t U`. With the two-sided type substitution approach: the fundamental lemma's conclusion at T_TAbs is about the term abstraction `tm_tabs t` under type substitutions σ1, σ2. The semantics at `Ty_All T1` (with σ1,σ2,ρ) is: for all W1 W2 R' fresh α: `SemTy (σ1[α↦W1]) (σ2[α↦W2]) (ρ[α↦R']) T1 (tm_tapp (tabs (t θ1 σ1)) W1, tm_tapp (tabs (t θ2 σ2)) W2)`. These step to `open_tm_ty (t θ1 σ1) W1` and `open_tm_ty (t θ2 σ2) W2`. Now the IH at fresh α with σ1' := σ1[α↦W1], σ2' := σ2[α↦W2], ρ' := ρ[α↦R'], θ1, θ2 gives: `SemTy σ1' σ2' ρ' (open_ty T1 α) ((open_tm_ty t α) θ1 σ1', (open_tm_ty t α) θ2 σ2')`. And we need this to equal the goal terms `open_tm_ty (t θ1 σ1) W1`. By the commuting lemma: `(open_tm_ty t α) θ1 σ1' = open_tm_ty (t θ1 σ1) (σ1' α) = open_tm_ty (t θ1 σ1) W1`. ✓!!! Because σ1' α = W1. Beautiful — the type substitution applied to the opened term at α gives exactly the W1-instantiated term. The α is only a placeholder that gets substituted away.

And the *type* side: `SemTy σ1' σ2' ρ' (open_ty T1 α)` should equal `SemTy σ1 σ2 ρ (open_ty T1 W1 / W2?)`... hmm, the goal's type is `open_ty T1 W1` on the left and `open_ty T1 W2` on the right. But SemTy is a *single* relation indexed by a *single* type T with two substitutions σ1, σ2. So we need `SemTy σ1' σ2' ρ' (open_ty T1 α) = SemTy σ1 σ2 ρ (open_ty T1 ?)`... hmm, but open_ty substitutes a bvar with a *single* type, whereas the two sides need different types. Hmm! Right: `open_ty T1 U` uses one type U for both sides. In the two-sided setting, the type on the left is `(open_ty T1 α)[σ1']` = `open_ty T1 W1` and on the right `(open_ty T1 α)[σ2']` = `open_ty T1 W2`. So the relation is `SemTy σ1' σ2' ρ' (open_ty T1 α)`, not `SemTy σ1 σ2 ... (open_ty T1 W1)` for a single W. So the correct semantic relation for the instantiated type is indexed by the syntactic type `open_ty T1 α` with *different substitutions* σ1', σ2' on the two sides. 

And in the T_TApp case: hypothesis gives `SemTy σ1 σ2 ρ (Ty_All T1)(...)`; unfolding the universal semantics: for all W1 W2 R', `SemTy σ1[α↦W1] σ2[α↦W2] ρ[α↦R'] T1 (tapp s1 W1, tapp s2 W2)`. Goal: `SemTy σ1 σ2 ρ (open_ty T1 U) (tapp s1 U, tapp s2 U)`. Note the goal has a SINGLE type U for both sides (because the typing rule T_TApp substitutes the same U on both sides). Now the semantics of `open_ty T1 U` under σ1, σ2: this is a type with BVar 0 replaced by U; the relation `SemTy σ1 σ2 ρ (open_ty T1 U)` should equal `SemTy σ1 σ2 ρ' (T1)` where ρ' has BVar 0 ↦ `SemTy σ1 σ2 ρ U` and fvars as ρ. Hmm, we need the BVar substitution lemma for SemTy: `SemTy σ1 σ2 ρ (open_ty T1 U) = SemTy σ1 σ2 (R_U ::? ) T1`. But SemTy's BVar handling: how do we handle free BVars? Use a list Rs as before. So define `SemTy (Rs : list rel) (σ1 σ2 : atom -> ty) (ρ : atom -> rel) (T : ty)`. Then the BVar lemma: `SemTy Rs σ1 σ2 ρ (open_ty T U) = SemTy (SemTy [] σ1 σ2 ρ U :: Rs) σ1 σ2 ρ T` for T locally closed at level 1 and U closed (under σ1,σ2? hmm, U's fvars get substituted by σ1/σ2, so U closed means locally closed type with fvars). ✓. Common approach for CBV logical relations:
- Define `V_T` on values (closed values).
- Define `E_T(t)` := t -->* v for some value v with V_T(v).
- For arrow: V_{A→B}(f) := forall v, V_A(v) -> E_B(f v).

With binary relations:
- `V_T ⊆ tm × tm` on values.
- `E_T(t1,t2) := exists v1 v2, t1 -->* v1 ∧ t2 -->* v2 ∧ value v1 ∧ value v2 ∧ V_T(v1,v2)`.

But for the ∀ case, `V_∀(f1,f2) := forall W1 W2 R', E_{T1[α]}(f1 W1, f2 W2)`? The IH gives E_{open_ty T1 α} for the α-opened body under environments. Hmm, but for the relation to be closed under type substitution... Let's see whether E and V definitions avoid the mismatch: In T_TAbs, we need `E_{T1[α]}? (f1 W1, f2 W2)` where f1 = tm_tabs body1. This reduces to `open_tm_ty body1 W1`. So if Sem = E (term-level interpretation), we're fine: the goal `E (f1 W1, f2 W2)` follows by forward closure of E from the IH's `E(opened1, opened2)`.

Good, the lemma holds. Need U closed (lc_ty_at 0 U implies lc_ty_at k U for all k).

Similarly for terms: wf_ty of the term's annotations and typing context give lc of the term? Actually we need lc_tm_at 0 0 of substituted terms. The fundamental lemma gives E which includes lc. Let's carefully enumerate the syntactic conditions needed in each step lemma:

- ST_AppAbs: requires `locally_closed_tm (tm_abs T t)` and `value v`; result `open_tm t v`.
- ST_App1: `t1 --> t1'`, `locally_closed_tm t2`; result `tm_app t1 t2 --> tm_app t1' t2`.
- ST_App2: `value v1`, `t2 --> t2'`.
- ST_TAppTabs: `locally_closed_tm (tm_tabs t)`, `locally_closed_ty T`.
- ST_TApp: `t --> t'`, `locally_closed_ty T`.
- ST_ChoiceLeft/Right: `locally_closed_tm t1`, `locally_closed_tm t2`.

For the "E_B(f1 a1, f2 a2)" from V_{A→B}(f1,f2), we apply f1 to a1 and f2 to a2, where f1,f2 are values (so lc), and a1,a2 are values in V_A (so lc). Then `f_i a_i` is a term; to reduce it we need ST_AppAbs: lc (tm_abs ...) — f_i is a value of arrow type, which must be an abstraction `tm_abs Ti bodyi` — but is it locally closed? `value (tm_abs T body)` gives `locally_closed_tm (tm_abs T body)` by the value constructor. ✓.

For the T_App case, reducing `s1 t1 -->* f1 a1` requires: s1 -->* f1 with each step via ST_App1 using lc t1. So we need lc t1. E_A(t1,t2) should include lc. Then `f1 t1 -->* f1 a1` via ST_App2 (value f1) ✓.

For T_TApp: s1 -->* f1 (type abstraction value) then `f1 U --> ...`; ST_TApp along s1 -->* f1 requires lc U (type). Then ST_TAppTabs gives `f1 U --> open_tm_ty body1 U` requiring lc (tm_tabs body1) — from value. So we need lc_ty U for the type argument in the relation quantification. Where does U come from in T_TApp? It's the inferred type argument `U` from the typing rule, which has `wf_ty Delta U`; we need lc_ty_at 0 U under the substitutions σ1, σ2 — i.e., `lc_ty_at 0 U` (U has no BVars; fvars substituted). Actually wf_ty implies lc_ty_at 0 for the *original* U (with fvars); substituting fvars doesn't change BVars. So lc_ty_at 0 U. But in the semantics `Sem [] ρ (open_ty T1 U)`, the universal relation's type quantifications... For the T_TAbs soundness, the universal relation quantifies W1, W2 with lc_ty_at 0 W1/W2, needed for ST_TAppTabs when reducing `tapp (tabs body) W`. ✓.

So the plan is feasible but heavy. Let's now think about how to state the fundamental lemma to avoid term-substitution complexities.

The fundamental lemma needs a *pair* of term substitutions θ1, θ2 (for the two sides) and a *pair* of type substitutions σ1, σ2. Let's define them as functions `nat -> tm`? Term variables are atoms (nat). Let's define:
- `tsub : atom -> tm` for term substitutions.
- `tysub : atom -> ty` for type substitutions.

Define the simultaneous substitution functions:
```
Fixpoint subst_tm (θ : atom -> tm) (σ : atom -> ty) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => θ x
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs (subst_ty σ T) (subst_tm θ σ t1)
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app ...
  | tm_tabs t1 => tm_tabs (subst_tm θ σ t1)
  | tm_tapp t1 T => tm_tapp (subst_tm θ σ t1) (subst_ty σ T)
  | tm_choice t1 t2 => tm_choice ...
  end.
```
Hmm wait, `tm_tabs t1`: the type variable bound here is a BVar; substituting fvars σ inside is fine (no capture, since σ maps fvars to types that may contain fvars, and the binder is a BVar — no capture of fvars! Because binders bind BVars, and fvars are always free). So type substitution of fvars in types is capture-free w.r.t. BVars? `ty_subst` replaces fvars by types; the replacement types may contain BVars, which could be captured by enclosing Ty_All binders! Direct definition (no E/V split):
`Sem Rs ρ (Ty_FVar α) := ρ α`
`Sem Rs ρ (Ty_Arrow A B) := fun s1 s2 => forall v1 v2, value v1 -> value v2 -> Sem Rs ρ A (v1,v2) -> Sem Rs ρ B (tm_app s1 v1, tm_app s2 v2)`

`Sem Rs ρ (Ty_BVar i) := nth i Rs ...`

Alright, this is a substantial but standard development. Let's also consider: do we need `Δ` at all in the semantics? The fundamental lemma quantifies over σ1, σ2 and ρ; the typing context Δ is only needed for freshness in T_TAbs/T_Abs. Let's settle the definition.

Option A (BVar-based body):
`Sem Rs ρ (Ty_All A) := fun s1 s2 => forall W1 W2 R', lc_ty_at 0 W1 -> lc_ty_at 0 W2 -> Sem (R'::Rs) ρ A (tm_tapp s1 W1, tm_tapp s2 W2)`.

Then T_TAbs soundness: goal for all W1 W2 R' (lc), `Sem (R'::[]) ρ T1 (tapp (tabs body1) W1, tapp (tabs body2) W2)`. Reduce (ST_TAppTabs, needs lc of the tabs terms and lc W) to `Sem (R'::[]) ρ T1 (open_tm_ty body1 W1, open_tm_ty body2 W2)`. IH at fresh α, σ1' = σ1[α↦W1], σ2' = σ2[α↦W2], ρ' = ρ[α↦R'] gives `Sem [] ρ' (open_ty T1 α) ((open_tm_ty body α)⟦θ1,σ1'⟧, ...)`. Now we need the BVar-vs-fvar opening lemma: `Sem [] ρ' (open_ty T1 α) = Sem (R'::[]) ρ T1` (as relations), given ρ' α = R' and α ∉ fvars(T1) etc. Wait, the lemma would be `Sem Rs ρ (open_ty T α) = Sem (ρ α :: Rs) ρ T` provided α is... hmm, need `ρ' α = R'` and for other fvars of T1, ρ' agrees with ρ; and ρ' = ρ[α↦R']. So `Sem [] ρ' (open_ty T1 α) = Sem (ρ' α :: []) ρ' T1 = Sem (R'::[]) ρ' T1 = Sem (R'::[]) ρ T1` (if α ∉ fvars(T1)). ✓.

And the terms: `(open_tm_ty body α)⟦θ1,σ1'⟧ = open_tm_ty (body⟦θ1,σ1⟧) W1` by the commuting lemma. ✓.

So Option A works with the fvar-opening lemma for types (proven by induction on types) and the term-substitution/opening commuting lemma.

Option B (fvar-based body): `Sem Rs ρ (Ty_All A) := fun s1 s2 => forall α R', Sem (R'::Rs) ρ A (tm_tapp s1 (Ty_FVar α), tm_tapp s2 (Ty_FVar α))`. Then T_TApp soundness: goal `Sem [] ρ (open_ty T1 U)(tapp s1 U, tapp s2 U)`. By the BVar-substitution lemma: `Sem [] ρ (open_ty T1 U) = Sem (Sem [] ρ U :: []) ρ T1`. Hypothesis with α := some fresh atom, R' := Sem [] ρ U gives `Sem (R'::[]) ρ T1 (tapp s1 α, tapp s2 α)`. But the goal terms are `tapp s1 U` — mismatch (α vs U). So Option A is better because T_TApp's goal term exactly matches the BVar-based relation (the type argument U matches the substituted type). Interesting: Option A's universal relation is type-based for the term instantiation and BVar-based for the body type. So T_TApp uses the hypothesis at W1=W2=U directly. ✓. And T_TAbs uses the IH at the fvar α and converts via the fvar-opening lemma. 

Let's now double check Option A at the "E" level. The V relation is defined by recursion on types for values; the E relation wraps it. But the universal case V_∀(f1,f2) needs to apply the terms to types W1,W2 and get an E relation at the body. Let's define:

```
Fixpoint V (Rs : list rel) (ρ : atom -> rel) (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => nth i Rs (fun _ _ => False) v1 v2
  | Ty_FVar α => ρ α v1 v2
  | Ty_Arrow A B => forall w1 w2, value w1 -> value w2 -> V Rs ρ A w1 w2 -> E Rs ρ B (tm_app v1 w1) (tm_app v2 w2)
  | Ty_All A => forall W1 W2 R', lc_ty_at 0 W1 -> lc_ty_at 0 W2 -> E (R'::Rs) ρ A (tm_tapp v1 W1) (tm_tapp v2 W2)
  end
with E (Rs) (ρ) (T) (s1 s2 : tm) : Prop :=
  exists v1 v2, value v1 /\ value v2 /\ s1 -->* v1 /\ s2 -->* v2 /\ V Rs ρ T v1 v2.
```

Hmm, mutual fixpoint with E calling V and V calling E (at arrow/all). The structural recursion: V on T calls E on subterms A, B; E on T calls V on T (same T). Coq's mutual fixpoint requires the recursive calls to be on structurally smaller arguments. V T calls E A where A is a subterm of T in the arrow/all case ✓. E T calls V T — not smaller! So a mutual fixpoint won't be accepted. Standard solution: define E as a *non-recursive* wrapper around a parameter, and define V by recursion on T where the recursion uses E at smaller types:

```
Fixpoint V (Rs) (ρ) (T) : tm -> tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Arrow A B => fun v1 v2 => forall w1 w2, value w1 -> value w2 -> V Rs ρ A w1 w2 -> E_def (V Rs ρ B) (tm_app v1 w1) (tm_app v2 w2)
  ...
```
where `E_def (R : tm -> tm -> Prop) (s1 s2) := exists v1 v2, value v1 ∧ value v2 ∧ s1 -->* v1 ∧ s2 -->* v2 ∧ R v1 v2`. Since V Rs ρ A is a recursive call on the subterm, and E_def takes the relation as a parameter, this is structurally recursive. ✓. Then define `E T s1 s2 := E_def (V T) s1 s2`.

Hmm, but in the Ty_All case: `E (R'::Rs) ρ A (tapp v1 W1) (tapp v2 W2)` uses V at A with the extended list — structurally smaller ✓.

Then the semantic type relation `Sem T s1 s2 := E_def (V T) s1 s2` for closed-ish T; and maybe we want Sem for open types with free BVars — we can just use V with the list.

Now the fundamental lemma statement. Let's define the term substitution function and state:

```
Definition tysubst_ty (σ : atom -> ty) (T : ty) : ty := ...  (* fvar substitution *)
Definition subst_tm (θ : atom -> tm) (σ : atom -> ty) (t : tm) : tm := ...
```

Fundamental lemma:
```
Lemma fundamental : forall Delta Gamma t T,
  has_type Delta Gamma t T ->
  forall σ1 σ2 ρ θ1 θ2,
  (forall x A, lookup_context x Gamma = Some A -> E [] ρ A (θ1 x) (θ2 x)) ->
  E [] ρ T (subst_tm θ1 σ1 t) (subst_tm θ2 σ2 t).
```

Hmm, but wait: the E relation at T uses ρ for fvars, and T's fvars are from Δ. But the *term* substitution σ1, σ2 also substitute those fvars in the term's types. There's no consistency requirement between ρ and σ1, σ2 — the relation is type-blind, so it doesn't matter. But there is a subtle issue: the environment condition and the conclusion use ρ, while θ may be arbitrary. That's fine.

But hold on: we also need conditions on θ for the lc requirements! The E relation includes lc of the terms (if we define it that way). Let's decide whether E includes lc. If E includes lc (`lc_tm_at 0 0 s1 ∧ lc_tm_at 0 0 s2 ∧ exists...`), then the fundamental lemma's conclusion gives lc of substituted terms, which we use for value-ness in the T_TAbs/T_Abs cases. Let's check the T_Abs case: we need `value (tm_abs (σ1 T1) body1)`. We have lc of the whole abstraction from... hmm, the E relation at the arrow type includes lc of `tm_abs (σ1 T1) body1`. Great. Now, the hard part is the formalization. Let's enumerate the syntactic lemmas needed:

**Term/type substitution basics:**
- `subst_ty` (fvar→ty) definition, `subst_tm` (fvar→tm, fvar→ty) definition.
- Identity: `subst_ty id_ty T = T`, `subst_tm id_tm id_ty t = t`.
- Composition/agreement: if σ and σ' agree on the fvars of T, then subst_ty σ T = subst_ty σ' T. Similarly for terms.
- Free variables: define `fvars_ty : ty -> list atom`? Or use a predicate `ty_fvar_occurs`. We need freshness conditions in the T_TAbs case: α ∉ fvars of T0, of the term's type annotations, of Γ's types. We can define `ty_fvars : ty -> list atom` and `tm_ty_fvars : tm -> list atom`. Or simpler: in the T_TAbs case, choose α fresh w.r.t. a finite list L' computed from the relevant objects. We can define a function to collect all atoms from types/terms, or use the fact that the premise holds for all α ∉ L; we just need α ∉ L plus a finite set S; L ∪ S is finite, so there exists an atom outside (use `exists_fresh` style with a bound: max of the list + 1). We'll need a helper: for any finite list of atoms, there's an atom not in it (e.g., max + 1). Since atoms are nats, this is easy.

**Well-formedness → local closure:**
- `wf_ty Δ T -> lc_ty_at 0 T`.
This is used for annotations (wf_ty Δ T1 in T_Abs, wf_ty Δ U in T_TApp) and for the target's U.

**Fundamental lemma** (as described). Let's write it precisely:

```
Lemma fundamental : forall Delta Gamma t T,
  has_type Delta Gamma t T ->
  forall sigma1 sigma2 rho theta1 theta2,
  (forall a, lc_ty_at 0 (sigma1 a)) ->
  (forall a, lc_ty_at 0 (sigma2 a)) ->
  (forall x, lc_tm_at 0 0 (theta1 x)) ->
  (forall x, lc_tm_at 0 0 (theta2 x)) ->
  (forall x A, lookup_context x Gamma = Some A -> E [] rho A (theta1 x) (theta2 x)) ->
  E [] rho T (subst_tm theta1 sigma1 t) (subst_tm theta2 sigma2 t).
```

Now let's think about the V/E definitions and the well-foundedness of the Fixpoint. In Coq:

```
Definition E_def (R : tm -> tm -> Prop) (s1 s2 : tm) : Prop :=
  exists v1 v2, value v1 /\ value v2 /\ s1 -->* v1 /\ s2 -->* v2 /\ R v1 v2.

Fixpoint V (Rs : list (tm -> tm -> Prop)) (rho : atom -> tm -> tm -> Prop) (T : ty) {struct T} : tm -> tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => match nth_error Rs i with Some R => R | None => fun _ _ => False end
  | Ty_FVar X => rho X
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => fun v1 v2 =>
      forall w1 w2, value w1 -> value w2 -> V Rs rho T1 w1 w2 ->
        E_def (V Rs rho T2) (tm_app v1 w1) (tm_app v2 w2)
  | Ty_All T1 => fun v1 v2 =>
      forall U1 U2 R', lc_ty_at 0 U1 -> lc_ty_at 0 U2 ->
        E_def (V (R' :: Rs) rho T1) (tm_tapp v1 U1) (tm_tapp v2 U2)
  end.
```
The recursive calls are on T1, T2 (subterms) ✓ so the Fixpoint is structurally decreasing ✓.

Define `E Rs rho T s1 s2 := E_def (V Rs rho T) s1 s2`.

Hmm, but in the Ty_Arrow case, the recursive call `V Rs rho T2` appears inside E_def as an argument. That's a recursive call applied to a subterm ✓, and E_def is a defined constant — the termination checker should accept (recursive occurrences in the body, applied to structurally smaller args). Actually, Coq's guard checker requires the recursive call's principal argument to be a variable or constructor pattern that is structurally smaller. `V Rs rho T2` with T2 a subterm of the match ✓. And V Rs rho T1 ✓.

For Ty_BVar: nth_error Rs i gives option; fine.

Note: in the Ty_All case, `V (R' :: Rs) rho T1` — recursive call on T1 ✓.

Now the substitution lemma statement:

```
Lemma V_subst : forall Rs k U R_U rho T,
  lc_ty_at 0 U ->
  R_U = V [] rho U ->   (* hmm, as a relation equality *)
  forall s1 s2,
    V (firstn k Rs ++ R_U :: skipn k Rs) rho T s1 s2 <->
    V Rs rho (open_ty_rec k U T) s1 s2.
```
Hmm, `R_U = V [] rho U` — as functions, use `forall s1 s2, R_U s1 s2 <-> V [] rho U s1 s2` to avoid Prop extensionality. Let's state it as a hypothesis `HRU : forall s1 s2, R_U s1 s2 <-> V [] rho U s1 s2`, and in the BVar case where i = k we need V Rs' rho U s1 s2 ↔ V [] rho U s1 s2, which is the "lc type independence of the list" lemma (Rs' is the list at that depth: `firstn k Rs ++ ...`? hmm, in the induction the list at the substitution position is R_U itself...). Let me re-examine: In the BVar case with i = k, LHS = nth i (firstn k Rs ++ R_U :: skipn k Rs) = R_U (since i = k). RHS = V Rs rho U. So we need R_U ↔ V Rs rho U; given HRU : R_U ↔ V [] rho U, and lc U → V Rs rho U ↔ V [] rho U (list-independence for lc types) ✓.

In the Ty_All case: LHS = forall W1 W2 R', lc W1 -> lc W2 -> E_def (V (R' :: (firstn k Rs ++ R_U :: skipn k Rs)) rho T1) (tapp v1 W1, tapp v2 W2). RHS = forall W1 W2 R', ... E_def (V (R'::Rs) rho (open_ty_rec (k+1) U T1)) (...). We need V (R' :: (firstn k Rs ++ R_U :: skipn k Rs)) rho T1 = V (R'::Rs) rho (open_ty_rec (k+1) U T1) — IH with k+1 and list R'::Rs: `firstn (k+1) (R'::Rs) ++ R_U :: skipn (k+1) (R'::Rs) = R' :: (firstn k Rs ++ R_U :: skipn k Rs)` ✓ by computation (needs the cons case of firstn/skipn to reduce; `firstn (S k) (a::l) = a :: firstn k l` holds definitionally in Coq ✓, same for skipn).

But careful: the IH needs HRU : R_U ↔ V [] rho U — unchanged ✓.

Then the corollary for fvar-opening:
```
Lemma V_open_fvar : forall Rs rho T X s1 s2,
  V (rho X :: Rs) rho T s1 s2 <-> V Rs rho (open_ty T (Ty_FVar X)) s1 s2.
```
This follows from V_subst with k=0, U = Ty_FVar X, R_U = rho X; need HRU: rho X ↔ V [] rho (Ty_FVar X) = rho X ✓ trivial; lc_ty_at 0 (Ty_FVar X) ✓.

And the BVar-substitution corollary for k=0 with U = U and R_U := V [] rho U:
```
Lemma V_open : forall Rs rho T U s1 s2,
  lc_ty_at 0 U ->

```
Wait, careful: open_ty T U = open_ty_rec 0 U T. ✓. But in the T_TApp case we need the *E* version: `E (V [] rho U :: []) rho T0 s1 s2 ↔ E [] rho (open_ty T0 U) s1 s2`. Since E = E_def ∘ V, this follows from V_open ✓. Hmm, but in T_TApp the goal type is `open_ty T0 U` where T0 is the *body* of the all type, and we need T0 to be lc at level 1 for the substitution? Let's double check: V_subst's induction at Ty_All works for any T1, no lc needed ✓. At BVar i with i ≠ k: nth i of the inserted list equals nth i Rs ✓. With i = k: uses HRU and list-independence of U (lc U ✓). So no lc condition on T needed. Good.

But wait, there's a subtle issue: the substitution at position k in a list, but open_ty_rec k U replaces BVar k at depth 0 and BVar k+1 at depth 1 etc. In list terms, at depth d, the list is extended with d binders at the front, so the position corresponding to BVar k at depth 0 becomes position k+d, and the list at depth d is `R_d::...::R_1::Rs`, so `firstn (k+d) (...) ++ R_U :: ...` — our induction handles this by the k+1 shift ✓. Good.

Now the "list independence for lc types" lemma:
```
Lemma V_lc_list : forall T, lc_ty_at 0 T -> forall Rs Rs' rho s1 s2, V Rs rho T s1 s2 <-> V Rs' rho T s1 s2.
```
As discussed, need the generalized version:
```

```
Hmm, using nth_error equality for all i < k. Proof by induction on T (generalizing k):
- BVar i: lc_ty_at k (BVar i) → i < k → nth_error equal → V = nth i ✓.
- FVar ✓.
- Arrow: IH on T1, T2 with the same k and hypothesis ✓.

So we need the IH at S k with the extended lists ✓. Then E_def ✓.
  To use the IH, it must be stated for all k (generalize k in the induction). ✓.

Now the "rho agreement" lemma:

Now, the E_def congruence: if V relations are pointwise equivalent then E_def equivalent ✓ (trivial).

Also, E_def is monotone under forward steps:
```
Lemma E_def_step_l : forall R s1 s1' s2, s1 --> s1' -> E_def R s1' s2 -> E_def R s1 s2.
Lemma E_def_step_r : ...
Lemma E_def_multi_l : s1 -->* s1' -> E_def R s1' s2 -> E_def R s1 s2.
```
Proof by induction on the multi/step. ✓.

Also `V_to_E`: value v1 -> value v2 -> V Rs rho T v1 v2 -> E_def (V Rs rho T) v1 v2.

Now the multi/step congruence lemmas:
```
Lemma multi_tapp : forall s s' U, s -->* s' -> lc_ty_at 0 U -> tm_tapp s U -->* tm_tapp s' U.
Lemma multi_app_l : forall s s' u, s -->* s' -> lc_tm_at 0 0 u -> tm_app s u -->* tm_app s' u.
Lemma multi_app_r : forall v s s', value v -> s -->* s' -> tm_app v s -->* tm_app v s'.
Lemma multi_trans : forall s s' s'', s -->* s' -> s' -->* s'' -> s -->* s''.
```
✓.

Also we need `value_no_step : value v -> v --> s -> False` and `multi_value_eq : value v -> v -->* s -> s = v`.

Actually, the "capture" issue is about whether the *composition* equals the simultaneous substitution; but here we're composing a substitution of BVars (opening) and a substitution of fvars, which are disjoint, so they commute exactly. The capture concern arises for composition of two BVar substitutions (requires shifting), not for fvar vs bvar substitutions. ✓ So the commutation lemmas hold unconditionally. 

But careful: `subst_tm` also substitutes fvars in *types* (annotations) using σ. And `open_tm_ty` substitutes BVars in types. These commute as disjoint substitutions ✓.

However, the lc preservation lemmas need lc conditions to prevent capture (substituting a term with BVars into a term under binders would leave BVars that might become "bound" — lc_tm_at would then fail or misattribute). So the lc lemmas require θ, σ lc.

OK. Now let's also think: do we need the freshness of x in T_Abs for the *equality* `subst_tm θ[x↦w] σ t2 = subst_tm θ σ t2`?
```
Fixpoint subst_tm (theta : atom -> tm) (sigma : atom -> ty) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => theta x
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs (ty_subst_f sigma T) (subst_tm theta sigma t1)
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst_tm theta sigma t1) (subst_tm theta sigma t2)
  | tm_tabs t1 => tm_tabs (subst_tm theta sigma t1)
  | tm_tapp t1 T => tm_tapp (subst_tm theta sigma t1) (ty_subst_f sigma T)

  end.
```

Commutation with open_tm:
```
Lemma subst_tm_open_tm : forall theta sigma t u,
  subst_tm theta sigma (open_tm t u) = open_tm (subst_tm theta sigma t) (subst_tm theta sigma u).
```
Hmm, is this true as stated for arbitrary u? open_tm t u replaces BVars with u; subst substitutes fvars in both. Since the substitutions are on disjoint variable classes, they commute. Let's prove by induction on t, generalized over k:
`subst_tm θ σ (open_tm_rec k u t) = open_tm_rec k (subst_tm θ σ u) (subst_tm θ σ t)`.
Check case tm_abs: LHS = subst (tm_abs T (open_tm_rec (S k) u t1)) = tm_abs (σ T) (subst (open_tm_rec (S k) u t1)); RHS = open_tm_rec k (subst u) (tm_abs (σ T) (subst t1)) = tm_abs (σ T) (open_tm_rec (S k) (subst u) (subst t1)) ✓ by IH.
✓ (no lc conditions needed as an equation). Hmm, wait: the RHS's `open_tm_rec k (subst u) (subst t)` uses the substituted u; but LHS replaces BVars with u *before* substituting, so u's fvars also get substituted → subst u ✓. And u's BVars are unchanged by subst ✓. So equality holds ✓.

Commutation with open_tm_ty:
```
Lemma subst_tm_open_tm_ty : forall theta sigma t U,
  subst_tm theta sigma (open_tm_ty t U) =
  open_tm_ty (subst_tm theta sigma t) (ty_subst_f sigma U).
```

✓.

Also, in the T_TAbs case, `subst_tm θ σ' body` vs `subst_tm θ σ body` requires σ,σ' agree on tm_ty_fvars body, i.e., α ∉ tm_ty_fvars(body) ✓.

Let's define the fvars functions:
```
Fixpoint ty_fvars (T : ty) : list atom :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar _ => []
  | Ty_FVar X => [X]
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => ty_fvars T1 ++ ty_fvars T2
  | Ty_All T1 => ty_fvars T1
  end.

Fixpoint tm_fvars (t : tm) : list atom :=  (* term fvars *)
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => []
  | tm_fvar x => [x]
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_fvars t1
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_fvars t1 ++ tm_fvars t2
  | tm_tabs t1 => tm_fvars t1
  | tm_tapp t1 T => tm_fvars t1
  | tm_choice t1 t2 => tm_fvars t1 ++ tm_fvars t2
  end.

Fixpoint tm_ty_fvars (t : tm) : list atom :=  (* type fvars occurring in t *)
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => []
  | tm_fvar _ => []
  | tm_abs T t1 => ty_fvars T ++ tm_ty_fvars t1
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_ty_fvars t1 ++ tm_ty_fvars t2
  | tm_tabs t1 => tm_ty_fvars t1
  | tm_tapp t1 T => tm_ty_fvars t1 ++ ty_fvars T
  | tm_choice t1 t2 => tm_ty_fvars t1 ++ tm_ty_fvars t2
  end.

Fixpoint ctx_ty_fvars (Gamma : context) : list atom :=
  match Gamma with
  | [] => []
  | (_, T) :: G => ty_fvars T ++ ctx_ty_fvars G
  end.
```

And a lemma `In x (ty_fvars T) <-> occurs`? We might need: if `~ In α (ty_fvars T)` then substituting σ' and σ (agreeing except at α) gives the same. We'll prove the agreement lemmas directly by induction using the list membership.

  ```
  Lemma nth_insert : forall i k Rs (R_U d : tm -> tm -> Prop), k <= length Rs ->
  ```

Now let's think about the "value" requirement on the arguments in V_arrow: we apply with w1 = w2 = v, needing `value v` ✓ (given).

Now, one more potential issue: the V_∀ definition quantifies over `lc_ty_at 0 U1/U2`, and in the final proof we instantiate W1 = W2 = U. But wait, is the relation really type-blind in the presence of the *value* relation's universal case? Let me double check the universal case: V (Ty_All A) v1 v2 = forall W1 W2 R', lc W1 -> lc W2 -> E_def (V (R'::Rs) rho A) (tapp v1 W1, tapp v2 W2). The type arguments W1, W2 are chosen by the quantifier, not read from the term. The term's own annotations/type args are never inspected. The only places type syntax is inspected is in the *type index* (T), not the terms. ✓ So type-blindness holds: V Rs rho T is a relation on untyped terms.

Hmm, but actually, there's a subtle catch: the *reduction* relation step uses types: ST_TAppTabs requires lc of the type argument, and produces `open_tm_ty t T`. Similarly σ1' α = W1 ✓.

Let me carefully write Coq code.

```coq
(* ============================================================ *)
(* Infrastructure added for the proof                           *)
(* ============================================================ *)

(* ---------- fresh atoms ---------- *)
Fixpoint max_list (l : list atom) : atom :=
  match l with
  | [] => 0
  | x :: l' => Nat.max x (max_list l')
  end.

Definition fresh (l : list atom) : atom := S (max_list l).

Lemma max_list_ge : forall l x, In x l -> x <= max_list l.
Proof.
  induction l as [|a l IH]; intros x Hin; simpl in *.
  - destruct Hin.
  - destruct Hin as [-> | Hin].
    + apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + transitivity (max_list l); [apply IH, Hin | apply Nat.le_max_r].
Qed.

Lemma fresh_not_in : forall l, ~ In (fresh l) l.
Proof.
  intros l Hin. unfold fresh in Hin.
  pose proof (max_list_ge l _ Hin). lia.
Qed.
```

Then fvars:

```coq
Fixpoint ty_fvars (T : ty) : list atom :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar _ => []
  | Ty_FVar X => [X]
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => ty_fvars T1 ++ ty_fvars T2
  | Ty_All T1 => ty_fvars T1
  end.

Fixpoint tm_fvars (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => []
  | tm_fvar x => [x]
  | tm_abs _ t1 => tm_fvars t1
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_fvars t1 ++ tm_fvars t2
  | tm_tabs t1 => tm_fvars t1
  | tm_tapp t1 _ => tm_fvars t1
  | tm_choice t1 t2 => tm_fvars t1 ++ tm_fvars t2
  end.

Fixpoint tm_ty_fvars (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => []
  | tm_fvar _ => []
  | tm_abs T t1 => ty_fvars T ++ tm_ty_fvars t1
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_ty_fvars t1 ++ tm_ty_fvars t2
  | tm_tabs t1 => tm_ty_fvars t1
  | tm_tapp t1 T => tm_ty_fvars t1 ++ ty_fvars T
  | tm_choice t1 t2 => tm_ty_fvars t1 ++ tm_ty_fvars t2
  end.

Fixpoint ctx_ty_fvars (Gamma : context) : list atom :=
  match Gamma with
  | [] => []
  | (_, T) :: G => ty_fvars T ++ ctx_ty_fvars G
  end.
```

Substitution:
```coq
Fixpoint tysubst (sigma : atom -> ty) (T : ty) : ty :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => Ty_BVar i
  | Ty_FVar X => sigma X
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => Ty_Arrow (tysubst sigma T1) (tysubst sigma T2)
  | Ty_All T1 => Ty_All (tysubst sigma T1)
  end.

Fixpoint tsubst (theta : atom -> tm) (sigma : atom -> ty) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => theta x
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs (tysubst sigma T) (tsubst theta sigma t1)
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (tsubst theta sigma t1) (tsubst theta sigma t2)
  | tm_tabs t1 => tm_tabs (tsubst theta sigma t1)
  | tm_tapp t1 T => tm_tapp (tsubst theta sigma t1) (tysubst sigma T)
  | tm_choice t1 t2 => tm_choice (tsubst theta sigma t1) (tsubst theta sigma t2)
  end.

Definition id_ty : atom -> ty := fun X => Ty_FVar X.
Definition id_tm : atom -> tm := fun x => tm_fvar x.

Definition upd_ty (sigma : atom -> ty) (X : atom) (U : ty) : atom -> ty :=
  fun Y => if Nat.eqb Y X then U else sigma Y.

Definition upd_tm (theta : atom -> tm) (x : atom) (u : tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb y x then u else theta y.

Definition upd_rel (rho : atom -> tm -> tm -> Prop) (X : atom) (R : tm -> tm -> Prop) : atom -> tm -> tm -> Prop :=
  fun Y => if Nat.eqb Y X then R else rho Y.
```

Lemmas about eqb: I'll need `Nat.eqb` reasoning helpers. Let me create:
```coq
Lemma eqb_refl' : forall (n : nat), (n =? n) = true.
Proof. apply Nat.eqb_refl. Qed.
Lemma eqb_sym : forall n m, (n =? m) = (m =? n).
Proof.
  intros n m.
  destruct (Nat.eqb n m) eqn:E; destruct (Nat.eqb m n) eqn:E'.
  - reflexivity.
  - apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. apply Nat.eqb_neq in E'. subst; contradiction.
  - apply Nat.eqb_eq in E'. apply Nat.eqb_neq in E. subst; contradiction.
  - reflexivity.
Qed.
```
Hmm, `Nat.eqb_eq : forall n m, (n =? m) = true <-> n = m` ✓ and `Nat.eqb_neq : forall n m, (n =? m) = false <-> n <> m` ✓.

```coq
Lemma lc_tm_subst_gen : forall t K k theta sigma,
```

```coq
Lemma lc_ty_open_inv : forall T K U, lc_ty_at 0 U -> lc_ty_at K (open_ty_rec K U T) -> lc_ty_at (S K) T.
Proof.
  induction T; intros K U HU H; simpl in *.
  - (* BVar *) destruct (Nat.eqb K n) eqn:E.
    + apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. subst. constructor. lia.
    + apply Nat.eqb_neq in E. constructor. lia.
  - (* FVar *) constructor.
  - (* Arrow *) constructor; eauto.  (* need to destruct H as conjunction *)
  - (* All T1 *) simpl in H. apply lc_ty_all. apply IHT with (K := S K) (U := U). apply HU. apply H.
Qed.
```

In Coq, `lc_ty_at K (Ty_Arrow a b)` has exactly the constructor lc_ty_arrow, so `inversion H as [..]` or `destruct H` won't work directly since it's an inductive with one applicable constructor; `inversion H; subst` gives the premises. Let's use `inversion H; subst; clear H` to get H1, H2.

Let me now write the V definition and lemmas. One consideration: for the substitution lemma's nth reasoning, I'll use `nth` with default `rel_default`. Let me define:
```coq
Definition rel_top : tm -> tm -> Prop := fun _ _ => True.
Definition rel_bot : tm -> tm -> Prop := fun _ _ => False.
```
Which default? Since we always maintain k ≤ length Rs, the default is never used in the interesting cases, but the BVar case in the substitution lemma with i < k requires matching. Use rel_bot.

Now the V definition:
```coq
Definition E_def (R : tm -> tm -> Prop) (s1 s2 : tm) : Prop :=
  exists v1 v2, value v1 /\ value v2 /\ s1 -->* v1 /\ s2 -->* v2 /\ R v1 v2.

Fixpoint V (Rs : list (tm -> tm -> Prop)) (rho : atom -> tm -> tm -> Prop) (T : ty) {struct T} : tm -> tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => nth i Rs rel_bot
  | Ty_FVar X => rho X
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => fun s1 s2 =>
      forall v1 v2, value v1 -> value v2 -> V Rs rho T1 v1 v2 ->
        E_def (V Rs rho T2) (tm_app s1 v1) (tm_app s2 v2)
  | Ty_All T1 => fun s1 s2 =>
      forall U1 U2 R', lc_ty_at 0 U1 -> lc_ty_at 0 U2 ->
        E_def (V (R' :: Rs) rho T1) (tm_tapp s1 U1) (tm_tapp s2 U2)
  end.

Definition E (Rs : list (tm -> tm -> Prop)) (rho : atom -> tm -> tm -> Prop) (T : ty) : tm -> tm -> Prop :=
  fun s1 s2 => E_def (V Rs rho T) s1 s2.
```

Guard check: V's recursive calls: `V Rs rho T2` inside E_def argument ✓ T2 subterm; `V Rs rho T1` ✓; `V (R'::Rs) rho T1` ✓. Should pass.

```coq
Lemma E_def_iff : forall R R' s1 s2, (forall a b, R a b <-> R' a b) -> (E_def R s1 s2 <-> E_def R' s1 s2).
```

```coq
Lemma nth_insert : forall (i k : nat) (Rs : list (tm -> tm -> Prop)) (RU d : tm -> tm -> Prop),
  k <= length Rs ->
  nth i (firstn k Rs ++ RU :: skipn k Rs) d =
  if Nat.eqb i k then RU else nth i Rs d.
Proof.
  intros i k. revert i. induction k as [|k IH]; intros i Rs RU d Hle.
  - simpl. destruct i as [|i].
    + simpl. rewrite Nat.eqb_refl. reflexivity.
    + simpl. rewrite Nat.eqb_neq by lia. reflexivity.
  - destruct Rs as [|R Rs'].
    + simpl in Hle; lia.
    + simpl. destruct i as [|i].
      * simpl. rewrite Nat.eqb_neq by lia. reflexivity.
      * simpl. rewrite IH. 2: simpl in Hle; lia.
        simpl. destruct (Nat.eqb i k) eqn:E.
        -- rewrite E. rewrite Nat.eqb_refl. reflexivity.
           Hmm, need to relate (S i =? S k) with (i =? k).
```
Actually `Nat.eqb (S i) (S k)` reduces to `Nat.eqb i k`? Let's check the definition of Nat.eqb: `Nat.eqb n m = match n, m with 0,0 => true | S n', S m' => Nat.eqb n' m' | _, _ => false end`. So `Nat.eqb (S i) (S k)` simplifies to `Nat.eqb i k` ✓ definitionally (probably with simpl/cbn). So after `simpl`, the goal's RHS becomes `if Nat.eqb i k then RU else nth (S i) (R :: Rs') d`, which reduces to `if Nat.eqb i k then RU else nth i Rs' d` ✓.

Let me redo the S i case: goal after simpl (of firstn/skipn on cons and nth on cons):
`nth i (firstn k Rs' ++ RU :: skipn k Rs') d = if Nat.eqb i k then RU else nth i Rs' d` — which is exactly IH's conclusion with Rs' ✓. So `apply IH; simpl in Hle; lia`. Wait, the goal after simplification of the LHS: `nth (S i) ((R :: firstn k Rs') ++ RU :: skipn k Rs') d`. Hmm, `(R :: firstn k Rs') ++ RU :: skipn k Rs'` = `R :: (firstn k Rs' ++ RU :: skipn k Rs')` definitionally? `app (R :: l1) l2 = R :: app l1 l2` ✓ definitionally. So `nth (S i) (R :: (...)) d = nth i (...) d` ✓ simpl should do it. Then the goal is IH's conclusion ✓.

For i = 0 case: goal `nth 0 ((R :: firstn k Rs') ++ RU :: skipn k Rs') d = if (0 =? S k) then RU else nth 0 (R :: Rs') d`; LHS = R; RHS = nth 0 (R::Rs') = R ✓ since 0 =? S k = false. `simpl; rewrite Nat.eqb_neq by lia; reflexivity` — hmm, after simpl the LHS is R and RHS is `if (0 =? S k) then RU else R`. `simpl` might reduce Nat.eqb 0 (S k) to false directly ✓. So reflexivity after simpl.

Base case k=0: goal `nth i ([] ++ RU :: skipn 0 Rs) d = if i =? 0 then RU else nth i Rs d`; simpl: `nth i (RU :: Rs) d = ...`. destruct i: i=0: LHS = RU, RHS = RU ✓; i=S i': LHS = nth i' Rs, RHS = nth (S i') Rs ✓ (since S i' =? 0 = false simplifies) ✓.

Hmm, one issue: the `if Nat.eqb i k then RU else ...` — when I destruct/rewrite with equation E : Nat.eqb i k = true/false, the goal can be finished with `rewrite E; reflexivity` etc.

Now V_subst_gen:

```coq
Lemma V_subst_gen : forall T, forall k Rs U RU rho,
  k <= length Rs ->
  (forall s1 s2, RU s1 s2 <-> V [] rho U s1 s2) ->
  lc_ty_at 0 U ->
  forall s1 s2,
    V (firstn k Rs ++ RU :: skipn k Rs) rho T s1 s2 <->
    V Rs rho (open_ty_rec k U T) s1 s2.
Proof.
  induction T; intros k Rs U RU rho Hle HRU Hlc s1 s2; simpl.
  - (* BVar n *)
    destruct (Nat.eqb n k) eqn:E.
    + apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. subst n.
      rewrite nth_insert by assumption.
      rewrite Nat.eqb_refl.
      simpl. (* RHS: V Rs rho U *)
      apply HRU.
    + apply Nat.eqb_neq in E.
      rewrite nth_insert by assumption.
      (* goal: (if n =? k then RU else nth n Rs d) <-> V Rs rho (if k =? n then U else Ty_BVar n) *)
      rewrite E.
      destruct (Nat.eqb k n) eqn:E2.
      * apply Nat.eqb_eq in E2; subst; contradiction.
      * reflexivity.
```

Now the multi lemmas:

```coq
Lemma multi_trans : forall t1 t2 t3, t1 -->* t2 -> t2 -->* t3 -> t1 -->* t3.
```

```
Lemma lc_tm_mono : forall t K K' k k',
  K <= K' -> k <= k' -> lc_tm_at K k t -> lc_tm_at K' k' t.

  - (* bvar *) inversion H. apply lc_tm_bvar. lia.
  - (* fvar *) apply lc_tm_fvar.
  - (* abs *) inversion H; subst. apply lc_tm_abs.
    + eapply lc_ty_mono; [exact HK | assumption].
    + apply IHt with (K := K) (k := S k); [exact HK | lia | assumption].
  - (* app *) inversion H; subst. apply lc_tm_app.
    + apply IHt1 with (K := K) (k := k); assumption.
    + apply IHt2 with (K := K) (k := k); assumption.
  - (* tabs *) inversion H; subst. apply lc_tm_tabs.
    apply IHt with (K := S K) (k := k); [lia | exact Hk | assumption].
  - (* tapp *) inversion H; subst. apply lc_tm_tapp.
    + apply IHt with (K := K) (k := k); assumption.
    + eapply lc_ty_mono; [exact HK | assumption].
  - (* choice *) inversion H; subst. apply lc_tm_choice; [apply IHt1 with (K := K) (k := k) | apply IHt2 with (K := K) (k := k)]; assumption.
Qed.
```

```
  (forall X, In X (ty_fvars T) -> sigma X = sigma' X) ->
  tysubst sigma T = tysubst sigma' T.
```

`f_equal` gives the two component goals. So I'll use that approach.

Let me rewrite tsubst_agree:

```
Lemma tsubst_agree : forall theta theta' sigma sigma' t,
  (forall x, In x (tm_fvars t) -> theta x = theta' x) ->
  (forall X, In X (tm_ty_fvars t) -> sigma X = sigma' X) ->
  tsubst theta sigma t = tsubst theta' sigma' t.
Proof.
  induction t as [n | x | T1 t1 IHt1 | t1 IHt1 t2 IHt2 | t1 IHt1 | t1 IHt1 T1 | t1 IHt1 t2 IHt2];
    intros Ht Hs; simpl.
  - reflexivity.
  - apply Ht. simpl; auto.
  - f_equal.
    + apply (tysubst_agree sigma sigma' T1).
      intros X Hin. apply Hs. simpl. apply in_or_app. left. assumption.
    + apply IHt1.
      * intros y Hin. apply Ht. simpl. assumption.
      * intros X Hin. apply Hs. simpl. right. assumption.
  - f_equal.
    + apply IHt1.
      * intros y Hin. apply Ht. simpl. apply in_or_app. left. assumption.
      * intros X Hin. apply Hs. simpl. apply in_or_app. left. assumption.
    + apply IHt2.
      * intros y Hin. apply Ht. simpl. apply in_or_app. right. assumption.
      * intros X Hin. apply Hs. simpl. apply in_or_app. right. assumption.
  - f_equal.
    apply IHt1.
    + intros y Hin. apply Ht. simpl. assumption.
    + intros X Hin. apply Hs. simpl. assumption.
  - f_equal.
    + apply IHt1.
      * intros y Hin. apply Ht. simpl. assumption.
      * intros X Hin. apply Hs. simpl. apply in_or_app. left. assumption.
    + apply (tysubst_agree sigma sigma' T1).
      intros X Hin. apply Hs. simpl. apply in_or_app. right. assumption.
  - f_equal.
    + apply IHt1.
      * intros y Hin. apply Ht. simpl. apply in_or_app. left. assumption.
      * intros X Hin. apply Hs. simpl. apply in_or_app. left. assumption.
    + apply IHt2.
      * intros y Hin. apply Ht. simpl. apply in_or_app. right. assumption.
      * intros X Hin. apply Hs. simpl. apply in_or_app. right. assumption.
Qed.
```
Note: in the abs case, after f_equal, the two goals are `tysubst sigma T1 = tysubst sigma' T1` and `tsubst theta sigma t1 = tsubst theta' sigma' t1` ✓. Apply tysubst_agree directly (it's a function taking the hypothesis) ✓.

Let me write the semantics definitions and lemmas in dev.v.

Definitions:
```
Definition rel := tm -> tm -> Prop.
Definition rel_bot : rel := fun _ _ => False.

Definition E_def (R : rel) (s1 s2 : tm) : Prop :=
  exists v1 v2, value v1 /\ value v2 /\ s1 -->* v1 /\ s2 -->* v2 /\ R v1 v2.

Fixpoint V (Rs : list rel) (rho : atom -> rel) (T : ty) {struct T} : rel :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => nth i Rs rel_bot
  | Ty_FVar X => rho X
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => fun s1 s2 =>
      forall v1 v2, value v1 -> value v2 -> V Rs rho T1 v1 v2 ->
        E_def (V Rs rho T2) (tm_app s1 v1) (tm_app s2 v2)
  | Ty_All T1 => fun s1 s2 =>
      forall U1 U2 R', lc_ty_at 0 U1 -> lc_ty_at 0 U2 ->
        E_def (V (R' :: Rs) rho T1) (tm_tapp s1 U1) (tm_tapp s2 U2)
  end.

Definition E (Rs : list rel) (rho : atom -> rel) (T : ty) : rel :=
  fun s1 s2 => E_def (V Rs rho T) s1 s2.
```

```
  k <= length Rs ->
```

Let me write the fundamental lemma:

```
Lemma fundamental : forall Delta Gamma t T,
  has_type Delta Gamma t T ->
  forall sigma1 sigma2 rho theta1 theta2,
  (forall X, lc_ty_at 0 (sigma1 X)) ->
  (forall X, lc_ty_at 0 (sigma2 X)) ->
  (forall x, lc_tm_at 0 0 (theta1 x)) ->
  (forall x, lc_tm_at 0 0 (theta2 x)) ->
  (forall x A, lookup_context x Gamma = Some A -> E [] rho A (theta1 x) (theta2 x)) ->
  E [] rho T (tsubst theta1 sigma1 t) (tsubst theta2 sigma2 t).
Proof.
  intros Delta Gamma t T H.
  induction H; intros sigma1 sigma2 rho theta1 theta2 Hsig1 Hsig2 Hth1 Hth2 Henv.
  - (* T_Var *) simpl. apply Henv. assumption.
  - (* T_Abs *) ...
  ...
Qed.
```

```
Lemma V_list_agree_all : forall T Rs Rs' rho,
  (forall i, nth i Rs rel_bot = nth i Rs' rel_bot) ->
  forall s1 s2, V Rs rho T s1 s2 <-> V Rs' rho T s1 s2.

  induction T as [n | X | T1 IH1 T2 IH2 | T1 IH1]; intros Rs Rs' rho Hag s1 s2; simpl.
  - apply Hag.
  - reflexivity.
  - split; intros H v1 v2 Hv1 Hv2 HV.
    + apply E_def_congr with (R := V Rs rho T2).
      * intros a b. apply (IH2 Rs Rs' rho Hag a b).
      * apply H; auto. apply (proj1 (IH1 Rs Rs' rho Hag v1 v2)); assumption.
    + apply E_def_congr with (R := V Rs' rho T2).
      * intros a b. split; [apply (proj2 (IH2 ... a b)) | apply (proj1 ...)].
      * apply H; auto. apply (proj2 (IH1 ...)); assumption.

    + apply E_def_congr with (R := V (R' :: Rs) rho T1).
      * intros a b. apply (IH1 (R'::Rs) (R'::Rs') rho ltac:(intros i; destruct i; [reflexivity|simpl; apply Hag]) a b).
      * apply H; assumption.

Qed.
```

This is exactly like V_list_agree but without the lc restriction.
